.

Translation of SPARQL graph patterns into Datalog rules.

The translation essentially follows the idea presented by Polleres
(
39
)
, adapted to multisets by Angles and Gutierrez
(
7
)
, and improved by Hernández
(
24
)
.
Specifically, we cover the following issues:

(1)

It considers the cases where a filter condition is evaluated as error. Some solutions are lost when these cases are not considered.

(2)

It considers that the equality
X=Y
must be evaluated as true only if
X
and
Y
are bound. The translation is fixed by using the literal
\operatorname{eq}(X,Y)
instead of a built-in equality
X=Y
. Since, atom
\operatorname{eq}(X,Y)
is true only if
X
and
Y
are terms in the database, the translation of the filter-condition
X=Y
is not evaluated as true when
X
and
Y
are unbound.

Given a SPARQL graph pattern
P
, the function
\delta(P)
is introduced to transform
P
into a set of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
rules.
The definition of function
\delta
is given by the translation rules presented in Table
4
.
Note that this function implies a recursive translation of graph patterns into Datalog rules, as well as the generation of fresh predicates.
To do this, the application of function
\delta
over a graph
P
will be based on exploring the parse tree of
P
.

A
parse tree
for a graph pattern
P
is an ordered rooted tree whose internal nodes represent the graph pattern operators in
P
(i.e., AND, UNION, EXCEPT, FILTER, SELECT), and whose leaves represent the triple patterns of
P
.
Additionally, we will assume that the nodes will be enumerated using a post-order traversal of the tree, that is, for any node
n
, first visit the left subtree, then visit the right subtree, and finally visit the node
n
itself.

Given a graph pattern
P
and its parse tree
T
, the execution of the function
\delta
consists of applying the rules shown in Table
4
to each node in
T
, following their enumeration. Next we present an example of this procedure.

Example 6.11.

Let
Q
be the following SPARQL query asking for all people, the place where they live, and optionally the people they know:

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{AND}\,({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}}))

\displaystyle\operatorname{UNION}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (&({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{EXCEPT}\\[-4.0pt]
&\begin{aligned} (&\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}}\;{{?}{somewhere}}\\[-4.0pt]
&\operatorname{WHERE}\;(({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{AND}\,({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}}))))).\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

This SPARQL query is not normalized because both sides of the
\operatorname{UNION}
operator have different variables.
To normalize this query, we replace the right-hand operand of the
\operatorname{UNION}
pattern by a
\operatorname{SELECT}
clause, and include the projection variables
{{?}{person}}
,
{{?}{somewhere}}
and
{{?}{somebody}}
.
The resulting normalized query is the following:

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{AND}\,({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}}))

\displaystyle\operatorname{UNION}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (&\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}}\;{{?}{somewhere}}\;{{?}{somebody}}\\[-4.0pt]
&\operatorname{WHERE}\begin{aligned} (&({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{EXCEPT}\\[-4.0pt]
&\begin{aligned} (&\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}}\;{{?}{somewhere}}\\[-4.0pt]
&\operatorname{WHERE}\;(({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{AND}\,({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}})))))).\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

{forest}

Figure 4.
Parse tree of the normalized SPARQL graph pattern presented in Example
6.11
. Each node represents a graph pattern, and each edge represents a composition relationship between graph patterns. The nodes were enumerated using a post-order traversal of the tree.
In this figure:

P_{1}
is the triple pattern
({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})
,

P_{2}
is
({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}})
,

P_{3}
is
({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})
,

P_{4}
is
({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})
,
and

P_{5}
is
({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}})
.

Assume that
P
is the normalized SPARQL graph pattern presented above, and
T
is the parse tree of
P
shown in Figure
4
.
Hence, the application of the function
\delta(P)
results in the following Datalog rules:

p_{1}(?person,?somewhere)\leftarrow\operatorname{triple}(?person,\mathsf{livesIn},?somewhere)

p_{2}(?person,?somebody)\leftarrow\operatorname{triple}(?person,\mathsf{knows},?somebody)

p_{3}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)\leftarrow p_{1}(?person_{1},?somewhere),

p_{2}(?person_{2},?somebody),

\operatorname{comp}(?person_{1},?person_{2},?person)

p_{4}(?person,?somewhere)\leftarrow\operatorname{triple}(?person,\mathsf{livesIn},?somewhere)

p_{5}(?person,?somewhere)\leftarrow\operatorname{triple}(?person,\mathsf{livesIn},somewhere)

p_{6}(?person,?somebody)\leftarrow\operatorname{triple}(?person,\mathsf{knows},?somebody)

p_{7}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)\leftarrow p_{5}(?person_{1},?somewhere),

p_{6}(?person_{2},?somebody),

\operatorname{comp}(?person_{1},?person_{2},?person)

p_{8}(?person,?somewhere)\leftarrow p_{7}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)

p_{9}(?person,?somewhere)\leftarrow p_{4}(?person,?somewhere),\neg p_{8}(?person,?somewhere)

p_{10}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)\leftarrow p_{9}(?person,?somewhere),

\operatorname{null}(?somebody)

p_{11}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)\leftarrow p_{3}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)

p_{11}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)\leftarrow p_{10}(?person,?somewhere,?somebody)

Note that the predicate names are enumerated according to the parse tree shown in Figure
4
.
In this sense:
the rule
p_{1}(\dots)\leftarrow\dots
encodes the triple pattern
P_{1}
;
the same applies for the predicated names
p_{2}
,
p_{4}
,
p_{5}
and
p_{6}
;
the rule
p_{3}(\dots)\leftarrow\dots
encodes the pattern
(P_{1}\operatorname{AND}P_{2})
;
the rule
p_{7}(\dots)\leftarrow\dots
encodes the pattern
(P_{5}\operatorname{AND}P_{6})
;
the rule
p_{8}(\dots)\leftarrow\dots
encodes the pattern
(\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}},

{{?}{somewhere}}\operatorname{WHERE}P_{7})
;
the rule
p_{9}(\dots)\leftarrow\dots
encodes the pattern
(P_{4}\operatorname{EXCEPT}P_{8})
;
the rule
p_{10}(\dots)\leftarrow\dots
encodes the pattern
(\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}},

{{?}{somewhere}},

{{?}{somebody}}\operatorname{WHERE}P_{9})
;
finally, the two rules with predicate name
p_{11}
encode the graph pattern
(P_{3}\operatorname{UNION}P_{10})
.

Based on the function
\delta
, we define a general method to transform SPARQL queries into
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries.

Definition 6.12 (Function
f_{12}
).

Given a SPARQL query
Q
, the function
f_{12}(P)
returns a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(L,\Pi)
where
L
is the goal atom
p(\bar{P})
and
\Pi
is a Datalog program containing the rules produced by
\delta(Q)
(note that
Q
is a graph pattern).

6.1.3.
Translating query answers from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to SPARQL

Recall that a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer is a pair
(V,M)
where
V
is a set of variables and
M
is a multiset of substitutions. Additionally, a SPARQL query answer is a multiset of solution mappings
\Omega
.

The main difference between a multiset of substitutions
M
and a multiset of solution mappings
\Omega
is the representation of null values.
Given a substitution
\theta
and a variable
?X\in\operatorname{dom}(\theta)
, a null value is represented with the assignment
\theta(?X)=\bot
. On the other hand, for a solution mapping
\mu
, a null value for
?X
is represented by not including
?X
in the domain of
\mu
.
Given a Datalog substitution
\theta
, the function
\operatorname{NotNull}(\theta)
returns a SPARQL solution mapping
\mu
defined as follows: (i)
\operatorname{dom}(\mu)=\{x\in\operatorname{dom}(\theta)\mid\theta(x)\neq\bot\}
, that is, the variables of
\mu
are the variables in
\theta
whose assignment is not
\bot
; and, (ii) for every variable
?X\in\operatorname{dom}(\theta)
, if
\theta(?X)\neq\bot
then
\mu(?X)=\theta(?X)
.
Hence, every value different from
\bot
is translated into an unbound variable in the corresponding solution mapping.

Definition 6.13 (Function
h_{12}
).

Given a multiset of Datalog substitutions
\Theta
, the function
h_{12}(\Theta)
returns a multiset of SPARQL solution mappings
\Omega
defined as follows:
(i)

\operatorname{set}(\Omega)=\{\operatorname{NotNull}(\theta)\mid\theta\in\operatorname{set}(\Theta)\}
; and,
for each substitution
\theta\in\operatorname{set}(\Theta)
, it applies that

\operatorname{card}(\operatorname{NotNull}(\theta),\Omega)=\operatorname{card}(\theta,\Theta)
.
In other words, the cardinality of each solution mapping
\operatorname{NotNull}(\theta)
in
\Omega
is defined as the number of colored copies
\langle\theta,i\rangle
occurring in
\operatorname{coloring}(\Theta)
.

Lemma 6.14.

SPARQL can be simulated by
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
.

Proof.

We need to show that, using the functions defined above,
(f_{12},g_{12},h_{12})
is a simulation of SPARQL in
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
.
The proof is in the Claim
4
of the Appendix.
∎

6.2.
From
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to SPARQL

This section shows that Non-Recursive Multiset Datalog with Safe Negation (
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
) can be simulated by SPARQL.
To support this, we describe the following translation functions:

•

f_{21}
is the function that translates
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries into SPARQL queries;

•

g_{21}
is the function that translates
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
databases into SPARQL databases; and

•

h_{21}
is the function that translates SPARQL query answers into
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answers.

6.2.1.
Translating databases from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to SPARQL

In general terms, a fact
p(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
can be translated into a set of triples of the form
(u,\alpha_{i},c_{i})
where
u
is a fresh IRI that identifies the fact, and
\alpha_{i}
is a reserved IRI which allows to describe that constant
c_{i}
is in the position
i
of the fact
2
2

2

An option can be the use of properties
rdf:_1
,
rdf:_2
,
rdf:_3
,
\dots
, defined in the RDF Schema 1.1 vocabulary.
. Also recall that the semantics of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
relies on the notion of colored set of a multiset (see Section
4.2
), which is the set containing the colored copies of the element of the multiset. This idea is formalized next.

In what follows, we will assume that
A=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\dots\}
is an enumerable set of special IRIs used to codify positions in Datalog atoms,

\mathtt{NULL}
is a special IRI, and any Datalog constant
c
and name of predicate
p
has an equivalent RDF IRI term (excluding the aforementioned special IRIs) that we will denote with the same symbol
c
and
p
.

Definition 6.15 (Function
g_{21}
).

Assume that
the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
database
D
contains
n
copies of a fact
F
(namely
p(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
), and the
\operatorname{coloring}(D)
contains the colored copies
\langle F,1\rangle
, …,
\langle F,n\rangle
of fact
F
. For each colored copy
\langle F,i\rangle
of
F
, we assume the existence of a fresh IRI
u_{\langle F,i\rangle}
, which we use to identify the colored copy.
Similarly the subscript
⟨F,i⟩
is used to identify the colored copy of a constant or a predicate.

Then the function
g_{21}
applied to the multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts
D
, returns a set of RDF triples (i.e. an RDF graph) defined as

g_{21}(D)=\{(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})\}\bigcup_{\langle F,i\rangle\in\operatorname{coloring}(D)}\\
\{(u_{\langle F,i\rangle},\alpha_{0},p_{\langle F,i\rangle}),(u_{\langle F,i\rangle},\alpha_{1},(c_{1})_{\langle F,i\rangle}),\dots,(u_{\langle F,i\rangle},\alpha_{n},(c_{n})_{\langle F,i\rangle})\}.

Example 6.16.

Let
D
be the following
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
database:

D=\lBrace p(a,b),p(a,b),p(a,c),q(b,d,a),q(b,e,a)\rBrace.

Note that the fact
p(a,b)
occurs twice in the multiset
D
, so we need to generate the colored copies for this fact, namely
\langle p(a,b),1\rangle
and
\langle p(a,b),2\rangle
. In contrast, the fact
p(a,b)
occurs once, so we just generate a simple colored copy, that is,
\langle p(a,c),1\rangle
.
Then the data is translated for SPARQL as follows:

g_{21}(D)=\begin{aligned} \{&(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{\langle p(a,b),1\rangle},\alpha_{0},p),(u_{\langle p(a,b),1\rangle},\alpha_{1},a),(u_{\langle p(a,b),1\rangle},\alpha_{2},b),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{\langle p(a,b),2\rangle},\alpha_{0},p),(u_{\langle p(a,b),2\rangle},\alpha_{1},a),(u_{\langle p(a,b),2\rangle},\alpha_{2},b),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{\langle p(a,c),1\rangle},\alpha_{0},p),(u_{\langle p(a,c),1\rangle},\alpha_{1},a),(u_{\langle p(a,c),1\rangle},\alpha_{2},c),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{\langle p(b,d,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{0},q),(u_{\langle p(b,d,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{1},b),(u_{\langle p(b,d,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{2},d),(u_{\langle p(b,d,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{3},a),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{\langle p(b,e,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{0},q),(u_{\langle p(b,e,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{1},b),(u_{\langle p(b,e,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{2},d),(u_{\langle p(b,e,a),1\rangle},\alpha_{3},a)\}.\end{aligned}

Intuitively, the SPARQL database corresponding to the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
database
D
consists of a set of triples that describe each of the facts, and the inclusion of triple
(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})
allows to ensure that the SPARQL database is not empty. The need of this additional triple is explained next when describing the translation from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries to SPARQL.

6.2.2.
Translating queries from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to SPARQL

A notable difference between
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
and SPARQL is the way both languages define the scope of variables. In
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
, all variables in a rule are universally quantified, and they are not in the scope of the query. On the other hand, variables in a SPARQL query are divided into in-scope and non-in-scope (see Subsection
3.4
). To see this difference, consider the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(q(X,Y),\Pi)
where the program
\Pi
consists of the single rule
R=q(Y,Z)\leftarrow p(X,Z,Y)
. Notice that the variables in the goal of the query do not correspond to the variables in the head of the rule
R
. To simplify the translation, we rename variables in rules according to the goal of the query.

In this case, we rewrite
R
as the rule
R^{\prime}=q(X,Y)\leftarrow p(Z,Y,X)
.
Formally, given a literal
L=q(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and a rule
R
whose head is
q(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})
, the
renamed rule of
R
with respect to
L
, denoted
\operatorname{vr}(R,L)
, is the rule
R^{\prime}

that results from
R
by consistently renaming each variable
Y_{i}
as
X_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Let
L
be a positive literal
p(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and
\Pi
be a normalized
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
program. We define the function
\operatorname{gp}(L,\Pi)
which translates
L
into a SPARQL graph pattern. The function
\operatorname{gp}
is defined recursively as follows:

(1)

If predicate name
p
does not occur in the head of any rule of
\Pi
(i.e.,
p
is extensional), then
\operatorname{gp}(L,\Pi)
returns

\displaystyle\operatorname{SELECT}\overline{X}\;((?Y,\alpha_{0},p)\operatorname{AND}\,(?Y,\alpha_{1},?X_{1})\operatorname{AND}\cdots\operatorname{AND}\,(?Y,\alpha_{n},?X_{n})),

where
\overline{X}=\operatorname{var}(L)
and
?Y
is a fresh variable.

(2)

Otherwise, if
p
occurs in the head of the rules
\{R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\}
in
\Pi
(i.e.,
p
is intensional), then
\operatorname{gp}(L,{\Pi})
returns:

(T(\operatorname{vr}(R_{1},L))\operatorname{UNION}\cdots\operatorname{UNION}T(\operatorname{vr}(R_{n},L))),

where the operator
T(R)
is defined as follows:

•

If
R
is a projection rule
L\leftarrow L_{1}
then
T(R)
is
(\operatorname{SELECT}\overline{X}\;P_{1})
where
\overline{X}=\operatorname{var}(L)
and
P_{1}=\operatorname{gp}(L_{1},\Pi)
;

•

If
R
is a join rule
L\leftarrow L_{1},L_{2}
then
T(R)
is
(P_{1}\operatorname{AND}P_{2})
where
P_{1}=\operatorname{gp}(L_{1},\Pi)
and
P_{2}=\operatorname{gp}(L_{2},\Pi)
;

•

If
R
is a negation rule
L\leftarrow L_{1},\neg L_{2}
then
T(R)
is
(P_{1}\operatorname{EXCEPT}P_{2})
where
P_{1}=\operatorname{gp}(L_{1},\Pi)
and
P_{2}=\operatorname{gp}(L_{2},\Pi)
.

Note that, if there is just one rule
R_{1}
then
\operatorname{gp}(L,\Pi)
can be reduced to
T(R_{1})
(no need to rename variables).

Example 6.17.

Consider the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(q(X),\Pi)
where program
\Pi
consists of the rule
q(X)\leftarrow p(X,Y)
. Then,
\operatorname{gp}(q(X),\Pi)
is the SPARQL query

\operatorname{SELECT}{}?X\;((?U,\alpha_{0},p)\operatorname{AND}\,(?U,\alpha_{1},?X)\operatorname{AND}\,(?U,\alpha_{2},?Y)).

The function
\operatorname{gp}
is not enough to translate
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries to SPARQL queries. Recall that a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer is a pair
(V,M)
where
V
is a set of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
variables and
M
is a set of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitutions, and a SPARQL query answer is a multiset
\Omega
of SPARQL mappings. To conclude the translation, we need to define a function that, given a SPARQL query answer
\Omega
, returns a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer
(V,M)
. The issue is that we cannot compute the set
V
when the multiset
\Omega
is empty. For example, an empty
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
database
D
is translated as the SPARQL database consisting of the set of triples
\{(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})\}
(see Subsection
6.2.1
). The evaluation of the query
\operatorname{gp}(q(X),\Pi)
in Example
6.17
returns an empty multiset of mappings,
\Omega
, where the query answer to the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(q(X),\Pi)
is a pair
(\{X\},M)
such that
M
is an empty multiset of solutions. Hence, the SPARQL query answer
\Omega
does not contain the information needed to generate the set of variables
\{X\}
in the answer of the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query.

To solve the aforementioned issue of having an empty SPARQL query answer, we can extend the function
\operatorname{gp}
with a query that introduces the variables of the query. This is done using the additional triple
(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})
we introduced in the translation. Given a set of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
variables
V=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
we write
\operatorname{VarQuery(V)}
to denote the SPARQL pattern

(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?X_{1})\;\operatorname{AND}\,\cdots\,\operatorname{AND}\;(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?X_{n}).

The translation of a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query is then the union of the graph patterns computed by the functions
\operatorname{gp}
and
\operatorname{VarQuery}
.

Definition 6.18 (Function
f_{21}
).

Given a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
Q=(L,\Pi)
, the function
f_{21}(Q)
returns a SPARQL graph pattern
(\operatorname{gp}(L,\Pi)\operatorname{UNION}\operatorname{VarQuery}(\operatorname{var}(L)))
.

Example 6.19.

Consider the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(q(X),\Pi)
in Example
6.17
. Then,
f_{21}((q(X),\Pi))
is the following SPARQL graph pattern:

(\operatorname{SELECT}{}?X\;((?U,\alpha_{0},p)\operatorname{AND}\,(?U,\alpha_{1},?X)\operatorname{AND}\,(?U,\alpha_{2},?X)))\operatorname{UNION}\,(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?X).

The result of evaluating the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query on an empty set of facts
D
is the pair
(\{X\},M)
where
M
is an empty multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitutions, whereas the result of evaluating the graph pattern
f_{21}((q(X),\Pi))
on the SPARQL database
g_{21}(D)=\{(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})\}
is the SPARQL query answer
\Omega=\{\{?X\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}\}\}
. Intuitively, the mapping
\{?X\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}\}
does not codify a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitution, but the variables in the domain of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitutions.

6.2.3.
Translating query answers from SPARQL to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}

Recall that a SPARQL query answer is a multiset of solution mappings, and a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer is a pair
(V,M)
(where
V
is a set of variables and
M
is a multiset of substitutions).
Since a SPARQL solution mapping can be seen as a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitution, the translation from SPARQL mappings to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitutions does not require modifications, except for the mapping
\{?X_{1}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL},\dots,?X_{n}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}\}
which is used to codify the solution variables.

Definition 6.20 (Function
h_{21}
).

Let
\Omega
be a multiset of SPARQL solution mappings that includes a mapping
\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}}
with cardinality 1 where
\operatorname{dom}(\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}})=V
and
\mu(?X)=\mathtt{NULL}
for every variable
?X\in\operatorname{dom}(\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}})
, and for every mapping
\mu^{\prime}\in\Omega
it holds that
\operatorname{dom}(\mu^{\prime})=V
. The
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
solution for
\Omega
, denoted
h_{21}(\Omega)
, is the pair
(V,M)
where
M
is the multiset of substitutions
\theta
defined as follows:

(1)

Given an SPARQL mapping
\mu=\{?X_{1}\mapsto c_{1},\dots,?X_{n}\mapsto c_{n}\}
the corresponding
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitution for mapping
\mu
is the substitution
\theta_{\mu}=\{X_{1}\mapsto c_{1},\dots,X_{n}\mapsto c_{n}\}

where the
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
variable
X_{i}
corresponds to the SPARQL variable
?X_{i}
.

(2)

\operatorname{set}(M)=\{\theta_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\Omega\setminus\{\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}}\}\}
.

(3)

\operatorname{card}(\theta_{\mu},M)=\operatorname{card}(\mu,\Omega)
.

Lemma 6.21.

\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
can be simulated by SPARQL.

Proof.

This is a long but straightforward induction on Datalog queries using as hypothesis that
(f_{21},g_{21},h_{21})
is a simulation of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
in SPARQL. The details of this proof are in the appendix (Claim
5
).
∎

6.3.
SPARQL and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
have the same expressive power

Putting together the simulations among SPARQL and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
stated in this section, we get the following theorem:

Theorem 6.22.

SPARQL and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
have the same expressive power.

Proof.

The claim is based on the simulation of SPARQL with
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
(Lemma
6.14
) and the simulation of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
with MRA (Lemma
6.21
).
∎

7.
Equivalence between MRA and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}

This section presents the simulations that prove that Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA) and Non-Recursive Multiset Datalog with Safe Negation (
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
) have the same expressive power.
Specifically, we show that MRA can be simulated by
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
(Section
7.1
), and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
can be simulated by MRA (Section
7.2
).

7.1.
From MRA to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}

This section shows that Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA) can be simulated by Non-Recursive Multiset Datalog with Safe Negation (
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
).
To support this, we describe the following translation functions:

•

f_{32}
is the function that translates MRA queries into
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries;

•

g_{32}
is the function that translates MRA databases into
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
databases; and

•

h_{32}
is the function that translates
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answers into MRA query answers.

7.1.1.
Translating databases from MRA to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}

Recall that a MRA database is a set of relations (where each relation is a multiset of tuples), and a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
database is a multiset of facts. First, we define a method to translate a MRA relation into a multiset of facts. Then, we define a method to translate a set of MRA relations into a multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts.

Assume the existence of functions that map: MRA relation names to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
predicate names, MRA attributes to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
variables, and MRA constants to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
constants.
Given a relation schema
R
, we write
\vec{R}
to denote a tuple containing the attributes of
R
in lexicographical order.

Given a multiset relation
r
, defined over a relation schema
R
, with
\vec{R}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})
, the function
\Sigma(r)
returns a multiset of Datalog facts defined as follows: For each tuple
t
in
r
,
\Sigma(r)
contains a fact
f
of the form
p(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
where
p
is the image of
R
, every
c_{i}
is
t(A_{i})
, and the cardinality of
f
in
\Sigma(r)
is given by the cardinality of
t
in
r
.

Definition 7.1 (Function
g_{32}
).

Given a MRA database
D
, the function
g_{32}
returns a multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts
D^{\prime}
defined as follows:

(1)

For each MRA relation
r
in
D
,
D^{\prime}
contains the facts returned by
\Sigma(r)
;

(2)

For each constant
c
in
D
,
D^{\prime}
contains a fact
\operatorname{eq}(c,c)
.

Example 7.2.

Let
D
be an MRA dataset consisting in two relations
r
and
s
with respective relation schemas
R
and
S
with
\vec{R}=(A_{1},A_{2})
and
\vec{S}=(A_{1},A_{3})
, and defined as follows:

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{2}\mapsto a_{2}\},\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{2}\mapsto a_{2}\},\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{2}\mapsto a_{3}\}\rBrace,

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{3}\mapsto a_{4}\}\rBrace.

Then, the corresponding
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
dataset is the following:

g_{32}(D)=\lBrace p_{R}(a_{1},a_{2}),p_{R}(a_{1},a_{2}),p_{R}(a_{1},a_{3}),p_{S}(a_{1},a_{4}),\operatorname{eq}(a_{1},a_{1}),\operatorname{eq}(a_{2},a_{2}),\operatorname{eq}(a_{3},a_{3}),\operatorname{eq}(a_{4},a_{4})\rBrace,

where predicates
p_{R}
and
p_{S}
are the corresponding images for the relation schemas
R
and
S
.

Note that the multiset of Datalog facts
D^{\prime}
is defined over the vocabulary that includes as predicate names all the relation names in
D
, and as arity of the predicate name
R
the number of attributes of the relation name
R
.

7.1.2.
Translating queries from MRA to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}

Recall that a MRA query is a relational algebra expression, and a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query is a set of rules.

First, we need to provide a recursive method to reduce MRA selection formulas into atomic formulas.
Such method is based on the following equivalences where
E
is an MRA expression, and
\psi
,
\psi_{1}
, and
\psi_{2}
are selection formulas:

(7)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}}(E)

\displaystyle\equiv\sigma_{\psi_{2}}(\sigma_{\psi_{1}}(E)),

(8)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}}(E)

\displaystyle\equiv\sigma_{\psi_{1}\land\neg\psi_{2}}(E)\cup\sigma_{\neg\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}}(E)\cup\sigma_{\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}}(E),

(9)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\neg\psi}(E)

\displaystyle\equiv E\setminus\sigma_{\psi}(E).

The proof of the validity of the above equivalences follows directly from the semantics of the selection operator. In particular, Equivalence
8
is rather involved because separates the disjunction in a union of three disjoint multiset relations in order to preserve the cardinality of each solution. Using the above equivalence, we get the following lemma.

Lemma 7.3.

For every MRA expression
E
, there exists an equivalent MRA expression
E^{\prime}
satisfying that all selection formulas in
E^{\prime}
are atomic.

Proof.

The proof follows from induction in the number
k
of Boolean connectives in selection formulas occurring in an MRA expression
E
. The base case is
k=0
and thus all selection formulas are atomic. If
k>0
, then the expression includes a selection expression whose formula has either the form
\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}
,
\neg\psi
, or
\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}
. In the first two cases, equivalences
7
and
9
reduce by one of the Boolean connectives of the expression. In the third case, the consecutive application of equivalences
8
,
7
, and
9
(in that order) reduces by one the number of Boolean connectives. Hence, we produce an equivalent query with
k-1
Boolean connectives.
∎

Definition 7.4 (Function
f_{32}
).

Let
Q
be a MRA query (i.e. an MRA expression), where selection formulas are atomic (i.e., have no Boolean connectives). The function
f_{32}(Q)
returns a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(L,\Pi)
where
L
is a goal clause of the form
q(\vec{Q})
where
q
is a predicate name corresponding to
Q
,
\vec{Q}
are the variables corresponding to the attributes in the schema
\widehat{Q}
(sorted in lexicographical order), and
\Pi
is a set of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
rules (i.e. a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
program) created by applying recursively the rules shown in Table
5
.

Note that function
f_{32}
assigns a fresh predicate name to each operation in query
Q
by following a non-deterministic approach. Although it is not difficult to define deterministic ways (like in the translation from SPARQL to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
), we omit in how intensional predicate names are assigned.

Table 5.
Definition of function
\Gamma
which translates an MRA expression into a set of Datalog rules. Given a MRA expression
E
, the recursive function
\Gamma(E)
returns a set of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
rules where:
q_{i}(\bar{A})
is a positive literal related to the MRA expression
E_{i}
,
q_{i}
is a fresh predicate name,
\bar{A_{i}}
denotes a set of variables,
\vec{R}
denotes the attributes in schema
\widehat{R}
, sorted in lexicographical order.

E_{0}

\Gamma(E_{0})

where …

q_{0}(\bar{A}_{0})\leftarrow R(\vec{R})

\bar{A}_{0}=\vec{R}

q_{0}(\bar{A}_{0})\leftarrow q_{1}(\bar{A}_{1}),q_{2}(\bar{A}_{2})
;

\Gamma(E_{1})
;
\Gamma(E_{2})

\bar{A}_{0}=\bar{A}_{1}\cup\bar{A}_{2}

(E_{1}\cup E_{2})

q_{0}(\bar{A}_{0})\leftarrow q_{1}(\bar{A}_{1})
;

q_{0}(\bar{A}_{0})\leftarrow q_{2}(\bar{A}_{2})
;

\Gamma(E_{1})
;
\Gamma(E_{2})

\bar{A}_{0}=\bar{A}_{1}=\bar{A}_{2}

(E_{1}\setminus E_{2})

q_{0}(\bar{A}_{0})\leftarrow q_{1}(\bar{A}_{1}),\neg q_{2}(\bar{A}_{2})
;

\Gamma(E_{1})
;
\Gamma(E_{2})

\bar{A}_{0}=\bar{A}_{1}

\pi_{S}(E_{1})

;

\rho_{A/B}(E_{1})

;

\bar{A_{0}}=\bar{A}_{1}

7.1.3.
Translating query answers from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to MRA

Recall that a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer is a pair
(V,M)
where
V
is a set of variables, and
M
is a multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
substitutions. On the other hand, an MRA query answer is a multiset relation. Next, we define a function
h_{32}
which translates a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer into a MRA query answer.

Definition 7.5 (Function
h_{32}
).

Given a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer
A=(V,M)
, the function
h_{32}(A)
returns a multiset relation
r
where: the schema of
r
is given by the set of attributes
V
(assume a simple transformation of variables to attribute names); for each substitution
\theta
in
M
, there is a tuple
t
in
r
satisfying that
t(X)=\theta(X)
for every attribute
X\in\widehat{R}
, and
\operatorname{card}(t,r)=\operatorname{card}(\theta,M)
.

Lemma 7.6.

MRA can be simulated by
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{32}
,
g_{32}
,
h_{32}
be the functions described in Definition
7.1
, Definition
7.5
and Definition
7.4
respectively.
The proof of this theorem follows from the claim that
(f_{32}
,
g_{32}
,
h_{32})
simulates MRA in
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
by using induction in the structure of queries. The proof of this claim is in the appendix (Claim
6
).
∎

7.2.
From
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to MRA

This section shows that Non-Recursive Multiset Datalog with Safe Negation (
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
) can be simulated by Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA).
To support this, we describe the following translation functions:

•

f_{23}
is the function that translates
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries into MRA queries;

•

g_{23}
is the function that translates
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
databases into MRA databases; and

•

h_{23}
is the function that translates MRA query answers into
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answers.

7.2.1.
Translating databases from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to MRA

Recall that a database in
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
is a multiset of facts, and a database in MRA is a set of relations (where each relation is a multiset of tuples). First, we define a method to translate a multiset of facts with the same predicate name into a relation
r
. Let
M
be a multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts having the same predicate name, i.e. every fact in
M
has the form
p(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
. The function
\psi(M)
returns a MRA relation
r
where: the relation schema
\widehat{r}
of
r
is given by the relation name
p
and the set of attributes
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}
, where each attribute name has the form
att\_i
with
1\leq i\leq n
; for each fact
p(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
in
M
there is a tuples
t
in
r
satisfying that
t(A_{i})=t_{i}
.

Next, we define function
g_{23}
which allows translating a multiset of facts into a set of relations.

Definition 7.7 (Function
g_{23}
).

Let
M
be a multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts
M
(i.e. an
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
database), and
\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
are the predicate names in
M
. The function
g_{23}(M)
returns a set of relations (i.e. a MRA database)
\{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\}
where
r_{i}=\psi(M_{i})
such that
M_{i}
is the subset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts of
M
having the predicate name
p_{i}
.

Example 7.8.

Let
M
be the multiset of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
facts defined as follows:

M=\lBrace p_{1}(c_{1},c_{2}),p_{1}(c_{1},c_{2}),p_{1}(c_{1},c_{3}),p_{2}(c_{1},c_{4})\rBrace,

Then, the corresponding MRA dataset
g_{23}(M)
consists of the following relations
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, over relation schemas
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, and with attributes

\widehat{{R}_{1}}=\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
and
\widehat{{R}_{2}}=\{B_{1},B_{2}\}
:

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{A_{1}\mapsto c_{1},A_{2}\mapsto c_{2}\},\{A_{1}\mapsto c_{1},A_{2}\mapsto c_{2}\},\{A_{1}\mapsto c_{1},A_{2}\mapsto c_{3}\}\rBrace,

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{B_{1}\mapsto c_{1},B_{2}\mapsto c_{4}\}\rBrace.

7.2.2.
Translating queries from
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
to MRA

Recall that a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query is a set of rules, and a MRA query is a relational algebra expression.

Let
\Pi
be a normalized
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
program. We define, by mutual recursion, functions
\delta_{1}(L,\Pi)
and
\delta_{2}(r,\Pi)
to translate (respectively) literals and rules into MRA expressions.

Given a literal
L
in
\Pi
of the form
p(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, the function
\delta_{1}(L,\Pi)
is defined as follows:

(1)

If predicate name
p
does not occur in the head of any rule of
\Pi
, then
\delta_{1}(L,\Pi)
returns the MRA expression
\rho_{A_{1}/X_{1}}(\cdots\rho_{A_{n}/X_{n}}(R)\cdots)
where
R
is the relation name associated to
p
;

(2)

Otherwise, if
p
occurs in the head of the rules
\{r_{1},\dots,r_{m}\}
in
\Pi
, then
\delta_{1}(L,\Pi)
returns the MRA expression
(E_{1}\cup(E_{2}\cup(\cdots E_{m})\cdots)
where each
E_{i}
is a MRA expression returned by
\delta_{2}(r_{i},\Pi)
.

Given a rule
r
in
\Pi
, the function
\delta_{2}(r,\Pi)
is defined as follows:

•

If
r
is a projection rule
L_{0}\leftarrow L_{1}
then
\delta_{2}(r,\Pi)
returns the MRA expression
\pi_{S}(E)
where
S
is the set of variables
\operatorname{var}(L_{0})
and
E
is the MRA expression returned by
\delta_{1}(L_{1},\Pi)
;

•

If
r
is a join rule
L_{0}\leftarrow L_{1},L_{2}
then
\delta_{2}(r,\Pi)
returns the MRA expression
(E_{1}\Join E_{2})
where
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are the MRA expressions returned by
\delta_{1}(L_{1},\Pi)
and
\delta_{1}(L_{2},\Pi)
respectively;

•

If
r
is a negation rule
L_{0}\leftarrow L_{1},\neg L_{2}
then
\delta_{2}(r,\Pi)
returns the MRA expression
(E_{1}\setminus E_{2})
where
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are the MRA expressions returned by
\delta_{1}(L_{1},\Pi)
and
\delta_{1}(L_{2},\Pi)
respectively.

Definition 7.9 (Function
f_{23}
).

Given a normalized
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
Q=(L,\Pi)
where
L
is the goal clause, and
\Pi
a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
program, the function
f_{23}(Q)
returns a MRA query defined by
\delta_{1}(L,\Pi)
.

7.2.3.
Translating query answers from MRA to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}

Recall that a MRA query answer is a multiset relation, and a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer is a pair
(V,M)
where
V
is a set of variables, and
M
is a multiset of substitutions.
Since a MRA tuple can be seen (interpreted) also as a Datalog substitution, the translation from MRA tuples to Datalog substitutions requires essentially no modifications.
Next, we define a function
h_{23}
which transforms a MRA query answer into a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer.

Definition 7.10 (Function
h_{23}
).

Given a MRA relation
R
with schema
\widehat{R}=\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}
, the function
h_{23}(R)
returns a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answer
A=(V,M)
where:
V
is a set of variables
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
where variable
X_{i}
corresponds to attribute
A_{i}
(assume a simple transformation of attribute names to variable names); and, for each tuple
t
in
R
, there is a substitution
\theta
in
M
satisfying that
\theta(X_{i})=t(A_{i})
for every attribute
A_{i}\in\widehat{R}
, and
\operatorname{card}(\theta,M)=\operatorname{card}(t,R)
.

Lemma 7.11.

\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
can be simulated by MRA.

Proof.

This is a long but straightforward induction on Datalog queries using as hypothesis that
(f_{23},g_{23},h_{23})
is a simulation of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
in MRA. The details of this proof are in the appendix (Claim
7
).
∎

7.3.
MRA and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
have the same expressive power

Putting together the simulations among MRA and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
stated in this section, we get the following theorem:

Theorem 7.12.

MRA and
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
have the same expressive power.

Proof.

The claim is based on the simulation of MRA with
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
(Lemma
7.6
) and the simulation of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
with MRA (Lemma
7.11
).
∎

8.
Equivalence between MRA and SPARQL

This section presents the simulations that prove that Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA) and SPARQL have the same expressive power. Specifically, we show that MRA can be simulated by SPARQL (Section
8.1
), and SPARQL can be simulated by MRA (Section
8.2
).

8.1.
From MRA to SPARQL

This section shows that Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA) can be simulated by SPARQL.
To support this, we describe the following translation functions:

•

f_{31}
is the function that translates MRA queries into SPARQL queries;

•

g_{31}
is the function that translates MRA databases into SPARQL databases; and

•

h_{31}
is the function that translates SPARQL query answers into MRA query answers.

8.1.1.
Translating databases from MRA to SPARQL

Recall that a MRA database is a set of relations (where each relation is a multiset of tuples), and a SPARQL database is a set of triples.

Assume the existence of functions that map relation names to IRIs, relation attributes to IRIs, and tuples to IRIs.

Let
r
be a multiset relation,
t
be a tuple in
r
and
\{t^{1},\dots,t^{n}\}
be the set of colored copies of
t
where
n=\operatorname{card}(t,r)
. The function
\beta(t,r)
returns a set of RDF triples defined as follows: for each copy
t^{i}
of
t
,
\beta(t,r)
contains a triple
(\mathit{iri}\_t^{i},\mathit{iri}\_b,iri\_r)
where
\mathit{iri}\_t^{i}
is an IRI which identifies the tuple
t^{i}
,
iri\_r
is an IRI which identifies the relation
r
, and
\mathit{iri}\_b
is an IRI which describes that
\mathit{iri}\_t^{i}
is a tuple of
iri\_r
; and, for each attribute
A
in
\widehat{r}
,
\beta(t,r)
contains a triple of the form
(\mathit{iri}\_t^{i},\mathit{iri}\_A,\mathit{lit}\_A)
where
\mathit{iri}\_A
is an IRI which identifies the attribute
A
, and
\mathit{lit}\_A
is a literal equivalent to the value
t(A)
.
Hence, for each copy of a tuple
t
we create a set of RDF triples.

Definition 8.1 (Function
g_{31}
).

Given a MRA database
D
, the function
g_{31}(D)
returns a set of RDF triples
D^{\prime}
defined as follows:

•

For each multiset relation
r
in
D
, and for each tuple
t
in
r
,
D^{\prime}
contains the RDF triples returned by
\beta(t,r)
;

•

D^{\prime}
contains a triple
(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})
where
\mathtt{NULL}
is a special IRI. Like in the simulation of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
with SPARQL, the simulation of MRA with SPARQL uses this special triple to retrieve the variables that are attributes of the MRA query answer.

Example 8.2.

Let
D
be an MRA dataset consisting in two relations
r
and
s
with respective relation schemas
R
and
S
with
\vec{R}=(A_{1},A_{2})
and
\vec{S}=(A_{1},A_{3})
, and defined as follows:

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{2}\mapsto a_{2}\},\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{2}\mapsto a_{2}\},\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{2}\mapsto a_{3}\}\rBrace,

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{A_{1}\mapsto a_{1},A_{3}\mapsto a_{4}\}\rBrace.

Then, the corresponding SPARQL dataset is the following:

g_{31}=\begin{aligned} \{&(u_{1}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_b,\mathit{iri}\_{r}),(u_{1}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_A_{1},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{1}}),(u_{1}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_A_{2},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{2}}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{1}^{2},\mathit{iri}\_b,\mathit{iri}\_{r}),(u_{1}^{2},\mathit{iri}\_A_{1},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{1}}),(u_{1}^{2},\mathit{iri}\_A_{2},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{2}}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(u_{2}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_b,\mathit{iri}\_{r}),(u_{2}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_A_{1},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{1}}),(u_{2}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_A_{2},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{3}}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(v_{1}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_b,\mathit{iri}\_{s}),(v_{1}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_A_{1},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{1}}),(v_{1}^{1},\mathit{iri}\_A_{3},\mathit{lit}\_A_{a_{4}}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL})\},\end{aligned}

where
u_{1}^{1}
,
u_{1}^{2}
, and
u_{2}^{1}
correspond to the IRIs for the tuples in the multiset relation
r
and
v_{1}^{1}
correspond to the IRI of the tuple in the multiset relation
s
.

8.1.2.
Translating queries from MRA to SPARQL

Recall that a MRA query is a relational algebra expression and a SPARQL query is a graph pattern.

First, consider the following issue.
A query answer in MRA is a multiset relation
r
over a set of attributes
\widehat{r}
, whereas a query answer in SPARQL does not specify a set of variables for which solutions are defined.
For example, the evaluation of the triple pattern
(?X,?Y,?Z)
over an empty RDF graph results in an empty multiset
\Omega
. The reference to the variables is not carried in the SPARQL answer. Like with the simulation of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
with SPARQL, we need to define a SPARQL pattern to retrieve the answer variables.

Given an MRA expression
E
, with attributes
\widehat{E}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, we write
\operatorname{AttrQuery}(E)
to denote the SPARQL pattern
(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?X_{1})\;\operatorname{AND}\,\cdots\,\operatorname{AND}\;(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?X_{n}),
where, for
1\leq i\leq n
, variable
?X_{i}
is the corresponding SPARQL variable for the MRA attribute
X_{i}
.

Example 8.3.

Consider the MRA expression
r\Join s
where
\widehat{r}=\{X,Y\}
and
\widehat{s}=\{Y,Z\}
. Then,
\operatorname{AttrQuery}(E)=(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?X)\;\operatorname{AND}\,(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?Y)\;\operatorname{AND}\,(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},?Z)
, where
?X
,
?Y
, and
?Z
are the corresponding SPARQL variables for attributes
X
,
Y
, and
Z
.

Recall that a MRA query is an MRA expression, and a SPARQL query is a SPARQL graph pattern. We will show that every type of MRA expression can be translated to a specific type of SPARQL graph pattern. Table
6
shows the translation rules which are the basis for the following definition.

Definition 8.4 (Function
f_{31}
).

Given an MRA expression
E
, the function
f_{31}
returns a SPARQL graph pattern defined by
(\Upsilon(E)\operatorname{UNION}\operatorname{AttrQuery(E)})
.

Table 6.
Definition of function
\Upsilon
which translates an MRA expression into a SPARQL pattern.

MRA expression
E

SPARQL pattern
\Upsilon(E)

where …

R

(
\operatorname{SELECT}

{?X_{1}\cdots?X_{n}}

iri_{r}
is the IRI that identifies
R
,
?Y
is a variable used to

((?Y,\mathit{iri\_b},\mathit{iri\_r})\operatorname{AND}

\quad((?Y,\mathit{\operatorname{iri}\_A}_{1},?X_{1})\operatorname{AND}(\cdots\operatorname{AND}(?Y,\mathit{\operatorname{iri}\_A}_{2},?X_{n})\cdots)

match every tuple of
R
, and
?X_{i}
is a variable that corresponds to the attribute
A_{i}
in schema
\widehat{R}
.

(E_{1}\Join E_{2})

(P_{1}\operatorname{AND}P_{2})

and
P_{2}=\Upsilon(E_{2})
.

(E_{1}\cup E_{2})

.

.

and

is the set of variables corresponding to the attributes in

.

,
{{?}{X}}
is the variable that corresponds to attribute

,
?Y
is the variable that corresponds to attribute

, and
\operatorname{subs}_{?X/?Y}(P_{1})
denotes the renaming of variable
{{?}{X}}
with variable
{{?}{Y}}
in the SPARQL query
P_{1}
(see Appendix
A
).

\sigma_{\psi}(E_{1})

, and
\varphi
is a filter condition equivalent to the selection condition
\psi
.

Example 8.5.

Consider the MRA expression
E=R\Join S
where
\widehat{R}=\{A,B\}
and
\widehat{S}=\{B,C\}
. Then the corresponding SPARQL query
f_{31}(E)
is the following:

f_{31}(E)=\begin{aligned} (&\begin{aligned} (&(\operatorname{SELECT}\;\{{{?}{X}}_{A},{{?}{X}}_{B}\}\operatorname{WHERE}\;({{?}{Y}}_{1},\mathit{\operatorname{iri}\_A},{{?}{X}}_{A})\operatorname{AND}({{?}{Y}}_{1},\mathit{\operatorname{iri}\_B},{{?}{X}}_{B}))\;\operatorname{AND}\\[-4.0pt]
&(\operatorname{SELECT}\;\{{{?}{X}}_{B},{{?}{X}}_{C}\}\operatorname{WHERE}\;({{?}{Y}}_{1},\mathit{\operatorname{iri}\_B},{{?}{X}}_{B})\operatorname{AND}({{?}{Y}}_{1},\mathit{\operatorname{iri}\_C},{{?}{X}}_{C})))\;\operatorname{UNION}\end{aligned}\\[-4.0pt]
&((\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{A})\operatorname{AND}\,(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{A})\operatorname{AND}\,(\mathtt{NULL},\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{A}))).\end{aligned}

If
R=\lBrace\{A\mapsto a,B\mapsto b\},\{A\mapsto a,B\mapsto b\}\rBrace
and
S=\lBrace\{B\mapsto b,C\mapsto c\}\rBrace
, the answer to the SPARQL query over the corresponding translation of the MRA database
D
to an RDF graph is the following multiset:

\llbracket f_{31}{E}\rrbracket_{g_{31}(D)}=\lBrace\begin{aligned} &\{{{?}{X}}_{A}\mapsto a,{{?}{X}}_{B}\mapsto b,{{?}{X}}_{C}\mapsto c\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{{{?}{X}}_{A}\mapsto a,{{?}{X}}_{B}\mapsto b,{{?}{X}}_{C}\mapsto c\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{{{?}{X}}_{A}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{B}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{C}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}\}\rBrace.\end{aligned}

Otherwise, if
R
is empty then:

\llbracket f_{31}{E}\rrbracket_{g_{31}(D)}=\lBrace\begin{aligned} &\{{{?}{X}}_{A}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{B}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL},{{?}{X}}_{C}\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}\}\rBrace.\end{aligned}

Whereas the first two SPARQL mappings
\{{{?}{X}}_{A}\mapsto a,{{?}{X}}_{B}\mapsto b,{{?}{X}}_{C}\mapsto c\}
correspond are duplicates of the same MRA answer,
\{A\mapsto a,B\mapsto b,C\mapsto c\}
, the last mapping does not correspond to an answer, but encodes the attributes of the MRA query. By encoding the attributes of the MRA query, we can reconstruct the result MRA relation even in the case it is empty.

8.1.3.
Translating query answers from SPARQL to MRA

Recall that a query answer in SPARQL is a multiset of mappings, and a query answer in MRA is a multiset relation (i.e. a multiset of tuples). Intuitively, a multiset of mappings
\Omega
can be transformed into a MRA relation
r
where the attributes in
\widehat{r}
are the variables in the domain of
\Omega
. This notion is defined next.

Definition 8.6 (Function
h_{31}
).

Let
\Omega
be a multiset of mappings with
\operatorname{dom}(\mu)=V
for every mapping
\mu\in\Omega
, and that includes the mapping
\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}}
with
\operatorname{dom}(\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}})=V
,
\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}}({{?}{X}})=\mathtt{NULL}
for every variable
?X\in V
, . The function
h_{31}(\Omega)
returns a multiset relation
r
where:

•

For each variable
{{?}{X}}\in V
, the schema
\widehat{r}
includes the MRA attribute
A
corresponding to variable
{{?}{X}}
.

•

The tuple
t_{\mu}
corresponding to a mapping
\mu
with
\operatorname{dom}(\mu)=V
is the tuple with attributes
\widehat{r}
such that
t(A)=\mu({{?}{X}})
, for each MRA attribute
A\in\widehat{r}
corresponding to a variable
{{?}{X}}\in V
.

•

\operatorname{set}(r)=\{t_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\operatorname{set}(\Omega)\setminus\{\mu_{V\mapsto\mathtt{NULL}}\}\}
.

•

\operatorname{card}(t_{\mu},r)=\operatorname{card}(\mu,\Omega)

Lemma 8.7.

MRA can be simulated in SPARQL.

Proof.

Let
f_{31},g_{31},h_{31}
denote respectively the functions stated in definitions and
8.4
,
8.1
, and
8.6
. The proof of this theorem follows from the claim that
(f_{31},g_{31},h_{31})
simulates MRA in SPARQL by using induction in the structure of queries. The proof of this claim is in the appendix (Claim
8
).
∎

8.2.
From SPARQL to MRA

This section shows that SPARQL can be simulated by Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA).
To support this, we describe the following translation functions:

•

f_{13}
is the function that translates SPARQL queries into MRA queries;

•

g_{13}
is the function that translates SPARQL databases into MRA databases; and

•

h_{13}
is the function that translates MRA query answers into SPARQL query answers.

The translation presented here is inspired by the one presented by Cyganiak
(
15
)
. However, unlike Cyganiak, we do not use null values with the SQL semantics. Instead, we use a special constant, denoted
\bot
, used to codify unbound values.

8.2.1.
Translating databases from SPARQL to MRA

Recall that a SPARQL database is a set of RDF triples, and a MRA database is a set of multiset relations. The translation of a set of RDF triples
G
will produce three multiset relations (without duplicates):
\operatorname{Trip}
, which codifies the RDF triples in
G
;
\operatorname{Null}
, introduced to manage the unbound values of SPARQL; and
\operatorname{Comp}
, introduced to simulate the notion of compatibility between mappings.

Definition 8.8 (Function
g_{13}
).

Let
\bot
be a special constant.
Given a set of RDF triples
G
, the function
g_{13}(G)
returns a multiset relational database
D^{\prime}
containing the multiset relations
\operatorname{Trip}
,
\operatorname{Null}
, and
\operatorname{Comp}
defined as follows:

(1)

\widehat{\operatorname{Trip}}=\{S,P,O\}
,
\operatorname{set}(\operatorname{Trip})=\{\{S\mapsto s,P\mapsto p,O\mapsto o\}\mid(s,p,o)\in G\}
, and
\operatorname{card}(t,\operatorname{Trip})=1
for every tuple
t\in\operatorname{set}(\operatorname{Trip})
.

(2)

\widehat{\operatorname{Null}}=\{N\}
,
\operatorname{set}(\operatorname{Null})=\{\{N\mapsto\bot\}\}
, and
\operatorname{card}(\{N\mapsto\bot\},\operatorname{Null})=1
.

(3)

\widehat{\operatorname{Comp}}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A\}
,
\operatorname{set}(\operatorname{Comp})
includes the tuple
\{A_{1}\mapsto\bot,A_{2}\mapsto\bot,A\mapsto\bot\}
and all tuples of the form
\{A_{1}\mapsto a,A_{2}\mapsto a,A\mapsto a\}
,
\{A_{1}\mapsto\bot,A_{2}\mapsto a,A\mapsto a\}
, and
\{A_{1}\mapsto a,A_{2}\mapsto\bot,A\mapsto a\}
where
a
is an RDF term in
G
, and
\operatorname{card}(t,\operatorname{Comp})=1
for every tuple
t\in\operatorname{set}(\operatorname{Comp})
.

Example 8.9.

Let
G
be the RDF graph defined as follows

G=\{\begin{aligned} &(\mathsf{Alice},\mathsf{livesIn},\mathsf{Santiago}),(\mathsf{Alice},\mathsf{knows},\mathsf{Bob}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(\mathsf{Bob},\mathsf{livesIn},\mathsf{Santiago}),(\mathsf{Bob},\mathsf{knows},\mathsf{Carol}),\\[-4.0pt]
&(\mathsf{Carol},\mathsf{livesIn},\mathsf{Lima})\}.\end{aligned}

Then the data is translated for MRA as the database
g_{13}(G)
with the multiset relations
\operatorname{Trip}
,
\operatorname{Null}
, and
\operatorname{Comp}
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Trip}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \lBrace&\{S\mapsto\mathsf{Alice},P\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},O\mapsto\mathsf{Santiago}\},\{S\mapsto\mathsf{Alice},P\mapsto\mathsf{knows},O\mapsto\mathsf{Bob}\}\\[-4.0pt]
&\{S\mapsto\mathsf{Bob},P\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},O\mapsto\mathsf{Santiago}\},\{S\mapsto\mathsf{Bob},P\mapsto\mathsf{knows},O\mapsto\mathsf{Carol}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{S\mapsto\mathsf{Carol},P\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},O\mapsto\mathsf{Lima}\}\rBrace\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Null}

\displaystyle=\lBrace\{N\mapsto\bot\}\rBrace

\displaystyle\operatorname{Comp}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \lBrace&\{A_{1}\mapsto\bot,A_{2}\mapsto\bot,A_{3}\mapsto\bot\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice},A_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice},A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice},A_{2}\mapsto\bot,A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\bot,A_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice},A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{Alice}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},A_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},A_{2}\mapsto\bot,A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\bot,A_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn},A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{livesIn}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\quad\vdots\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima},A_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima},A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima},A_{2}\mapsto\bot,A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima}\},\\[-4.0pt]
&\{A_{1}\mapsto\bot,A_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima},A_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{Lima}\}\rBrace.\end{aligned}

8.2.2.
Translating queries from SPARQL to MRA

Recall that a SPARQL query is a graph pattern, and a MRA query is a relational algebra expression.
First, we define the function
\Lambda
which allows translating an RDF triple pattern into a MRA expression.

Assume that
a
,
b
,
c
are RDF terms, and
?X
,
?Y
,
?Z
are variables.
Recall that
\operatorname{Trip}
is a multiset relation that is obtained from a set of RDF triples, where
\widehat{\operatorname{Trip}}=\{S,P,O\}
is the schema of
\operatorname{Trip}
.
Given a triple pattern
T
, the function
\Lambda(T)
returns a MRA expression defined as follows
3
3

3

These rules are based on Cyganiak’s translation
(
15
)
.
:

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},b,c)
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{P=b\land O=c}(\operatorname{Trip})))
;

•

if
T
is
(a,{{?}{Y}},c)
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{Y}}}(\rho_{P/{{?}{Y}}}(\sigma_{S=a\land O=c}(\operatorname{Trip})))
;

•

if
T
is
(a,b,{{?}{Z}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{O/{{?}{Z}}}(\sigma_{S=a\land P=b}(\operatorname{Trip})))
;

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},{{?}{Y}},c)
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}},{{?}{Y}}}(\rho_{P/{{?}{Y}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{O=c}(\operatorname{Trip}))))
;

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},b,{{?}{Z}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}},{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{O/{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{P=b}(\operatorname{Trip}))))
;

•

if
T
is
(a,{{?}{Y}},{{?}{Z}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{Y}},{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{O/{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{P/{{?}{Y}}}(\sigma_{S=a}(\operatorname{Trip}))))
;

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},{{?}{Y}},{{?}{Z}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}},{{?}{Y}},{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{O/{{?}{Z}}}(\rho_{P/{{?}{Y}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\operatorname{Trip}))))
;

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},{{?}{X}},c)
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{S=P\land O=c}(\operatorname{Trip})))
;

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},b,{{?}{X}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{P=b\land S=O}(\operatorname{Trip})))
.

•

if
T
is
(a,{{?}{X}},{{?}{X}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}}}(\rho_{P/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{S=a\land P=O}(\operatorname{Trip})))
;

•

if
T
is
({{?}{X}},{{?}{X}},{{?}{X}})
then
\Lambda(T)
returns

\pi_{{{?}{X}}}(\rho_{S/{{?}{X}}}(\sigma_{S=P\land P=O}(\operatorname{Trip})))
;

Second, we define a function
\gamma
that allows translating a SPARQL filter condition into a MRA selection condition. Like in the translation from SPARQL to
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
, it is not necessary to translate complex filter conditions (SPARQL) to complex selection formulas (MRA) because SPARQL queries can be normalized to avoid logical connectives.

Given an atomic filter condition
\varphi
, the function
\gamma(\varphi)
is defined recursively as follows:

•

If
\varphi
is
{{?}{X}}=c
then
\gamma(\varphi)
is
(\neg(X=\bot)\land X=c)
where
X
is the attribute name corresponding to variable
{{?}{X}}
;

•

If
\varphi
is
{{?}{X}}={{?}{Y}}
then
\gamma(\varphi)
is
((\neg(X=\bot)\land\neg(Y=\bot))\land X=Y)
where
X
and
Y
are the attribute names corresponding to variables
{{?}{X}}
and
{{?}{Y}}
, respectively;

•

If
\varphi
is
\operatorname{bound}(X)
then
\gamma(\varphi)
is
\neg(X=\bot)
where
X
is the attribute name corresponding to variable
{{?}{X}}
.

In Definition
8.8
, we introduced the relation named
\operatorname{Comp}
to simulate the compatibility between mappings. For example, to simulate the SPARQL query
Q=(P_{1}\operatorname{AND}P_{2})
we need to ensure that check if two pairs of mappings
\mu_{1}\in\llbracket P_{1}\rrbracket_{G}
and
\mu_{2}\in\llbracket P_{2}\rrbracket_{G}
are compatible, and if they are compatible, return the mapping
\mu=\mu_{1}\cup\mu_{2}
resulting from joining them. To explain how this operation is simulated with MRA, let
\operatorname{inScope}(P_{1})\cap\operatorname{inScope}(P_{2})=\{{{?}{X}}\}
and tuples
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
correspond to mappings
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. To be compatible, either both mappings map variable
{{?}{X}}
to the same value, or at least for one of the mappings, variable
{{?}{X}}
is unbound. For tuples, an unbound variable
{{?}{X}}
is represented with an attribute value
\bot
(e.g.,
t(X)=\bot
). Then, to check if tuples
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are
compatible
, we need to rename the attribute name
X
corresponding to variable
{{?}{X}}
as two attributes, namely
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
and check if there exists a tuple
t_{3}
in the result of query
\rho_{A_{1}/X_{1}}(\rho_{A_{2}/X_{2}}(\operatorname{Comp}))
that agrees with tuples
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
(i.e.,
t_{3}(X_{1})=t_{1}(X)
and
t_{3}(X_{2})=t_{2}(X)
) or agrees with either
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
whereas for the other tuple the value is
\bot
(e.g.,
t_{3}(X_{1})=t_{1}(X)
and
t_{2}(X_{2})=\bot
). We recover the renamed attribute
X
for the attribute
A
in the relation named
\operatorname{Comp}
. That is, for the compatibility we use the MRA expression
\rho_{A/X}(\rho_{A_{1}/X_{1}}(\rho_{A_{2}/X_{2}}(\operatorname{Comp})))
which is generalized as follows for multiple common variables in the scope of patterns
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a finite set of attribute names, and
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
be two bijective functions that map each attribute
X\in\mathcal{X}
to two different sets of attributes (i.e., the ranges of
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are disjoint). Then, we write
\operatorname{Comp}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\mathcal{X})
to denote the join of MRA expressions of the form

\rho_{A/X}(\rho_{A_{1}/\nu_{1}(X)}(\rho_{A_{2}/\nu_{2}(X)}(\operatorname{Comp}))),

for every attribute name
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

Let
E
be a MRA expression, and
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
be a subset of the attribute names in
\widehat{E}
, and
\nu
a bijective function that maps each attribute name in
\mathcal{X}
to a fresh attribute name (i.e.,
\nu(X)\notin\widehat{E}
). We call
\nu(E)
to the MRA expression that renames each attribute name
X\in\mathcal{X}
with
\nu(X)
. That is,
\nu(E)=\rho_{X_{1}/\nu(X_{1})}(\cdots\rho_{X_{n}/\nu(X_{n})}(E)\cdots)
.

Given two MRA expressions
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, assume two bijective functions
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
that map each attribute
X\in\widehat{E}_{1}\cap\widehat{E}_{2}
to two fresh attributes (i.e.,
\nu_{1}(X),\nu_{2}(X)\notin\widehat{E}_{1}\cup\widehat{E}_{2}
), and satisfy
\operatorname{range}(\nu_{1})\cap\operatorname{range}(\nu_{2})=\emptyset
. Then, we define the MRA operation
E_{1}*E_{2}
in terms of existing MRA operators as follows:

E_{1}*E_{2}=\pi_{\widehat{E}_{1}\cup\widehat{E}_{2}}(\operatorname{Comp}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\widehat{E}_{1}\cap\widehat{E}_{2})\Join\nu_{1}(E_{1})\Join\nu_{2}(E_{2})).

Notice that the attribute names in the ranges of functions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
in the definition of expression
E_{1}*E_{2}
do not matter because are not in the schema of the multiset that results from expression
E_{1}*E_{2}
.

To translate SPARQL queries
Q
of the form
(\operatorname{SELECT}\;\mathcal{X}\;P)
where the set of variables
\mathcal{X}
include a variable that is not in the scope of
P
, we need to generate values
\bot
to fill the tuples returned by the translated query. For example, if
\operatorname{inScope}(P)=\{{{?}{X}}\}
and
\mathcal{X}=\{{{?}{X}},{{?}{Y}}\}
, then the MRA expression
E
that corresponds to the SPARQL pattern
P
can be extended with an attribute name
Y
by joining
E
with the MRA relation
\rho_{N/Y}(\operatorname{Null})
. Given a set
\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\}
of attribute names, we define the MRA expression
\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
as
\rho_{N/Y_{1}}(\operatorname{Null})\Join\cdots\Join\rho_{N/Y_{n}}(\operatorname{Null})
.

Next, we present the translation of SPARQL queries to MRA queries.

Definition 8.10 (Function
f_{13}
).

The translation rules in Table
7
define the function
f_{13}
from normalized graph patterns whose filter conditions have no Boolean connectives to MRA queries.

Table 7.
Definition of the function
f_{13}
, which takes a normalized SPARQL pattern
P
as input (without logical connectives in filter conditions) and returns an MRA query.

SPARQL pattern
P

MRA query
f_{13}(P)

where…

(s,p,o)

\Lambda(s,p,o)

(P_{1}\operatorname{AND}P_{2})

(f_{13}(P_{1})*f_{13}(P_{2}))

(P_{1}\operatorname{UNION}P_{2})

(f_{13}(P_{1})\cup f_{13}(P_{2}))

(P_{1}\operatorname{EXCEPT}P_{2})

(f_{13}(P_{1})\setminus f_{13}(P_{2}))

(\operatorname{SELECT}\operatorname{inScope}(P)\;P_{1})

\pi_{\mathcal{A}}(f_{13}(P_{1})\Join\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})

\mathcal{A}
is the set of attribute names corresponding to the variables in set
\operatorname{inScope}(P)
and
\mathcal{B}
is the set of attribute names that correspond to variables that are in set
\operatorname{inScope}(P)\setminus\operatorname{inScope}(P_{1})
.

(P_{1}\operatorname{FILTER}~\varphi)

\sigma_{\gamma(\varphi)}(f_{13}(P_{1}))

Example 8.11.

Let
Q
be the following SPARQL query asking for all people, the place where they live, and optionally the people their know (notice that this query is already normalized as we discussed in Example
6.11
).

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{AND}\,({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}}))

\displaystyle\operatorname{UNION}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (&\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}}\;{{?}{somewhere}}\;{{?}{somebody}}\\[-4.0pt]
&\operatorname{WHERE}\begin{aligned} (&({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{EXCEPT}\\[-4.0pt]
&\begin{aligned} (&\operatorname{SELECT}{{?}{person}}\;{{?}{somewhere}}\\[-4.0pt]
&\operatorname{WHERE}\;(({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})\operatorname{AND}\,({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}})))))).\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

Then, the corresponding query
f_{13}(Q)
is the query
(q(X),\Pi)
where
\Pi
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle(\Lambda({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})*\Lambda({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}}))\;\cup

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (&\begin{aligned} (&\pi_{\mathit{Person}}(\Lambda({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}}))\;\setminus\\[-4.0pt]
&\pi_{\mathit{Person}}(\Lambda({{?}{person}},\mathsf{livesIn},{{?}{somewhere}})*\Lambda({{?}{person}},\mathsf{knows},{{?}{somebody}})))\;\Join\end{aligned}\\[-4.0pt]
&\rho_{N/\mathit{Somebody}}(\operatorname{Null}))\,,\end{aligned}

where the MRA attributes
\mathit{Person}
and
\mathit{Somebody}
correspond to the SPARQL variables
{{?}{person}}
and
{{?}{somebody}}
.

8.2.3.
Translating query answers from MRA to SPARQL

Recall that a MRA query answer is a multiset of tuples, and a SPARQL query answer is a multiset of solution mappings.
Next, we define the function
h_{31}
that transforms MRA query answers into
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query answers.

Intuitively, the translation of a MRA tuple
t
as a SPARQL solution mapping
\mu
consists of removing from tuple
t
every attribute whose value is
\bot
, and viewing the result tuple as a SPARQL mapping
\mu
. For example, the result of translating a tuple
t
with
\hat{t}=\{X,Y\}
,
t(X)=a
, and
t(Y)=\bot
, is the SPARQL mapping
\mu=\{{{?}{X}}\mapsto a\}
. Recall that we write
{{?}{X}}
to denote the corresponding SPARQL variable for a MRA attribute
X
.

Definition 8.12 (Function
h_{31}
).

Given a MRA tuple
t
, we write
f_{31}(t)
to denote the SPARQL mapping
\mu
such that: (1)
\mu({{?}{X}})=t(X)
if
X\in\hat{t}
and
t(X)\neq\bot
, and (2) variable
{{?}{Y}}
is not in
\operatorname{dom}(\mu)
if
Y\notin\hat{t}
or
t(Y)=\bot
. Abusing notation,
f_{31}(r)
is also the function that receives a MRA relation
r
and returns the multiset
\Omega
of SPARQL mappings where
\operatorname{set}(\Omega)=\{\mu\mid\text{ there exist }t\in r\text{ such that }f_{31}(t)=\mu\}
and the cardinality of mapping
f_{31}(t)
in
\Omega
is the cardinality of tuple
t
in
r
.

Lemma 8.13.

SPARQL can be simulated by MRA.

Proof.

This is a long but straightforward induction on the structure of SPARQL queries using as hypothesis that
(f_{13},g_{13},h_{13})
is a simulation of SPARQL by MRA. The details of this proof are in the appendix (Claim
9
).
∎

8.3.
MRA and SPARQL have the same expressive power

Putting together the simulations among MRA and SPARQL stated in this section, we get the following theorem:

Theorem 8.14.

MRA and SPARQL have the same expressive power.

Proof.

The claim is based on the simulation of MRA with SPARQL (Lemma
8.7
) and the simulation of SPARQL with MRA (Lemma
8.13
).
∎

9.
Conclusions

We studied the algebraic and logic structure of the multiset semantics of the core SPARQL patterns, and compared it to the classical and well-studied formalisms of multiset relational algebra and multiset Datalog. Our motivation was to shed light on the underlying theoretical structure of the multiset features of SPARQL that could
help improve future designs and implementations.
In this regard, the main discoveries of this research are: (1) the core fragment of SPARQL patterns matches precisely the multiset semantics of Datalog as defined by Mumick et al.
(
37
)
; and (2) this logical structure corresponds to a simple multiset algebra, namely the Multiset Relational Algebra (MRA).
These correspondences, besides showing a nice parallel to the one exhibited by classical set relational algebra and relational calculus, and thus transferring theoretical guarantees from these well-studied formalisms, could
help to give new insights on possible optimizations and future extensions of SPARQL.

We think there are a couple of lessons learned in the investigation of the multiset features of SPARQL.
First, contrary to the rather chaotic variety of multiset operators in SQL, it is interesting to observe that the SPARQL design comprises a more coherent body of multiset operators.
We suggest that this asset should be considered and curated by designers in order to try to keep this clean design in future extensions of SPARQL.
Second, there is a challenging goal for query language designers that work with multisets: existing a diversity of multiset extensions for each of the classical set operators, it is not evident at all from a theoretical perspective how to develop a logically coherent formalism that could integrate all or most of them.

Our study shows that there are fragments that behave coherently, but that operators that do not fit in this schema, when available (not always), have to be accessed in a very ad-hoc manner.
Last but not least, this study shows (and adds evidence of) the complexities and challenges that the introduction of multisets brings to query languages, exemplified here in the case of SPARQL. Much more use cases are needed in order to match the theoretical restrictions and recommendations (e.g. as studied in this paper), and real-life use cases that to the best of our knowledge do not have yet a good systematization.

, grant SFB-1574-471687386.

Appendix A
Variable renaming in SPARQL

This appendix section defines function
\operatorname{subs}(\cdot,\cdot)
, which renames SPARQL variables. This function is used to simulate the MRA operator renaming
\rho_{A/B}
(see Table
6
). Note that function
\operatorname{subs}(\cdot,\cdot)
is not an additional algebraic operation but an operation over expressions (i.e., a query rewriting). Intuitively, given a MRA query
Q
, a SPARQL pattern
P
that simulates
Q
, a renaming of MRA attributes
A/B
and a renaming of variables
{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}
where
{{?}{X}}
and
{{?}{Y}}
are the corresponding variables for attributes
A
and
B
, the query rewriting
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)
simulates the MRA query
\rho_{A,B}(Q)
. To this end, SPARQL variables are renamed in the pattern, instead of renaming query result attributes as MRA does.

Definition A.1 (SPARQL Variable Renaming).

Given two SPARQL variables
{{?}{X}}
and
{{?}{Y}}
, we define the function
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}:\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{L}\cup\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{L}\cup\mathbf{V}
as the function such that
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}({{?}{X}})={{?}{Y}}
and
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(s)=s
, for every
s\in(\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{L}\cup\mathbf{V})\setminus\{{{?}{X}}\}
. Given a SPARQL pattern
P
and two SPARQL variables
{{?}{X}}\in\operatorname{inScope}(P)
and
{{?}{Y}}\notin\operatorname{inScope}(P)
, we write
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)
to denote the pattern defined recursively as follows:

(1)

If
P
is a triple pattern
(s,p,o)
then
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=(\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(s),\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(p),\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(o))
.

(2)

If
P
has the form
(P_{1}\operatorname{AND}P_{2})
then
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{1})\operatorname{AND}\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{2})
.

(3)

If
P
has the form
(P_{1}\operatorname{UNION}P_{2})
then
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{1})\operatorname{UNION}\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{2})
.

(4)

If
P
has the form
(P_{1}\operatorname{EXCEPT}P_{2})
then
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{1})\operatorname{EXCEPT}\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{2})
.

(5)

If
P
has the form
(P_{1}\operatorname{FILTER}\varphi)
then
\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=(\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P_{1})\operatorname{FILTER}\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi))
where, abusing of notation,
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi)
is the selection formula defined recursively as follows:

(a)

If
\varphi
has the form
a=b
, where
a,b\in\mathbf{V}\cup\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{I}
, then
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi)=\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(a)=\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(b)
.

(b)

If
\varphi
has the form
\operatorname{bound}(?x)
then
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi)=\operatorname{bound}(\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(?x))
.

(c)

If
\varphi
has the form
\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}
then
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi)=\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\psi_{1})\land\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\psi_{2})
.

(d)

If
\varphi
has the form
\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}
then
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi)=\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\psi_{1})\lor\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\psi_{2})
.

(e)

If
\varphi
has the form
\neg\psi
then
\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\varphi)=\neg\nu_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(\psi)
.

(6)

If
P
has the form
(\operatorname{SELECT}W\operatorname{WHERE}P_{1})
then:

(a)

If
{{?}{Y}}\notin\operatorname{inScope}(P_{1})
, then

\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=(\operatorname{SELECT}~(W\setminus\{{{?}{X}}\}\cup\{{{?}{Y}}\})\operatorname{WHERE}~P_{1})
.

(b)

Otherwise,

\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{X}}/{{?}{Y}}}(P)=(\operatorname{SELECT}~(W\setminus\{{{?}{X}}\}\cup\{{{?}{Y}}\})\operatorname{WHERE}~\operatorname{subs}_{{{?}{Y}}/Z}(P_{1}))
, where

{{?}{Z}}
is a fresh variable. We rename variable
{{?}{Y}}
as
{{?}{Z}}
when is not in-scope of
P
to avoid a variable name clash.
