\operatorname{\emph{true}}

Claim 1.

For every SPARQL formula
\varphi
, the formula
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
can be expressed as a formula of the form
\bigvee_{\psi\in C}\psi
where
C
is a non-empty set of conjunctions of formulas belonging to one of the following types:

(1)

positive or negative literals (i.e., formulas of the form
\operatorname{\emph{false}}
,
{{?}{X}}=a
,
\neg({{?}{X}}=a)
,
\neg({{?}{X}}={{{?}{Y}}})
,
\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})
, or
\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})
),

(2)

formulas
\varphi^{\prime}
,
\neg\varphi^{\prime}
, or
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi^{\prime})
such that
\varphi^{\prime}
occurs in
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
is strictly smaller than
\varphi
;

and for every mapping
\mu
,
\mu(\varphi)=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
if and only if there exists a unique formula
\psi\in C
for which
\mu(\psi)=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
.

Proof.

We next show this result by induction on the structure of the query.

(1)

If
\varphi
has the form
\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})
then
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is the formula
\operatorname{\emph{false}}
. Formula
\varphi
satisfies the claim. Indeed,
C=\{\operatorname{\emph{false}}\}
and
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi))=\operatorname{\emph{false}}
for every mapping
\mu
because formula
\varphi
does not produce error.

(2)

If
\varphi
has the form
{{?}{X}}=a
then
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is the formula
\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})
. Formula
\varphi
satisfies the claim. Indeed,
C=\{\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})\}
and
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi))=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
if and only if variable
{{?}{X}}
is unbound in
\mu
, that is the unique case when formula
\varphi
produces error.

(3)

If
\varphi
has the form
{{?}{X}}={{{?}{Y}}}
then
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is the formula
\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}\lor\psi_{3}
where
\psi_{1}
is the formula
(\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})\land\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}))
,
\psi_{2}
is the formula
(\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})\land\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}))
, and
\psi_{3}
is the formula
(\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})\land\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}))
. Formula
\varphi
satisfies the claim. Indeed,
C=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3}\}
, and by construction, only one formula in
C
can be true, and
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi))=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
if and only if
\mu(\varphi)=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
.

(4)

If
\varphi
has the form
\neg\varphi_{1}
then
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is the formula
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{1})
. In this case
C=\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{1})\}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{1}))=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
if and only if
\mu(\varphi_{1})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
. Because
\neg\operatorname{\emph{error}}
is
\operatorname{\emph{error}}
, we conclude that
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi))=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
if and only if
\mu(\varphi)=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
. Hence, formula
\varphi
satisfies the claim.

(5)

If
\varphi
has the form
\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
then
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is the formula
\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}\lor\psi_{3}
where
\psi_{1}
is the formula
(\varphi_{1}\land\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))
,
\psi_{2}
is the formula
(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{1})\land\varphi_{2})
, and
\psi_{3}
is the formula
(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{1})\land\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))
. The validity of the claim for formula
\varphi
is shown in Table
8
. There are three cases where
\mu(\varphi)=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
:

Case ET:

\mu(\varphi_{1})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
and
\mu(\varphi_{2})=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
,

Case TE:

\mu(\varphi_{1})=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
and
\mu(\varphi_{2})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
,

Case EE:

\mu(\varphi_{1})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
and
\mu(\varphi_{2})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
.

Note that if
\mu(\varphi_{1})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
and
\mu(\varphi_{2})=\operatorname{\emph{false}}
then
\mu(\varphi)=\operatorname{\emph{false}}
(because
\operatorname{\emph{error}}\land\operatorname{\emph{false}}
is
\operatorname{\emph{false}}
).

The values in the remaining columns can be computed using the inductive hypothesis. We next present case ET as an example. The other cases follow the same reasoning. In case ET,
\mu(\varphi_{1})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
and
\mu(\varphi_{2})=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{1}))=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
and
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))
is either
\operatorname{\emph{false}}
or
\operatorname{\emph{error}}
.

(a)

If
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))=\operatorname{\emph{false}}
then:

\displaystyle\mu(\psi_{1})

\displaystyle=\mu(\varphi_{1})\land\mu(Error(\varphi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{error}}\land\operatorname{\emph{false}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{false}}.

\displaystyle\mu(\psi_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu(Error(\varphi_{1}))\land\mu(\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{true}}\land\operatorname{\emph{true}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{true}}.

\displaystyle\mu(\psi_{3})

\displaystyle=\mu(Error(\varphi_{1}))\land\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{true}}\land\operatorname{\emph{false}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{false}}.

Hence,

\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi))=\mu(\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}\lor\psi_{3})=\operatorname{\emph{false}}\lor\operatorname{\emph{true}}\lor\operatorname{\emph{false}}=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
.

(b)

If
\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))=\operatorname{\emph{error}}
then:

\displaystyle\mu(\psi_{1})

\displaystyle=\mu(\varphi_{1})\land\mu(Error(\varphi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{error}}\land\operatorname{\emph{error}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{error}}.

\displaystyle\mu(\psi_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu(Error(\varphi_{1}))\land\mu(\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{true}}\land\operatorname{\emph{true}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{true}}.

\displaystyle\mu(\psi_{3})

\displaystyle=\mu(Error(\varphi_{1}))\land\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{true}}\land\operatorname{\emph{error}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\emph{error}}.

Hence,

\mu(\operatorname{Error}(\varphi))=\mu(\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}\lor\psi_{3})=\operatorname{\emph{error}}\lor\operatorname{\emph{true}}\lor\operatorname{\emph{error}}=\operatorname{\emph{true}}
.

(6)

If
\varphi
has the form
\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
then the validity of the claim for formula
\varphi
is shown in Table
9
, following the same reasoning as for the previous case where
\varphi
is a conjunction
\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
.

∎

B.2.
Reduction of complex filter conditions

To prove the following claims, we introduce the notion of
reduction
and
reducible filter condition
. Section
6.1.2
presents three equivalences to transform a pattern with complex filter conditions into a pattern where all filter conditions are atomic. In this appendix, we show that these equivalences can be used to this end. For each equivalence
(P\operatorname{FILTER}\varphi)\equiv P^{\prime}
, we define a function that maps the filter condition
\varphi
to the set
\Sigma_{\varphi}
of filter conditions in pattern
P^{\prime}
.

Consider the following equivalences:

\displaystyle(P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})\equiv((P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{1})\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{2}),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2})\equiv\;&(P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})\operatorname{UNION}\\[-5.0pt]
&(P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{1}\land\neg\psi_{2})\operatorname{UNION}\\[-5.0pt]
&(P\operatorname{FILTER}\neg\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2})\operatorname{UNION}\\[-5.0pt]
&(P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi_{1}\land\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}))\operatorname{UNION}\\[-5.0pt]
&(P\operatorname{FILTER}\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})\land\psi_{2}),\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle(P\operatorname{FILTER}\neg\psi)\equiv((P\operatorname{EXCEPT}\,(P\operatorname{FILTER}\psi))\operatorname{EXCEPT}\,(P\operatorname{FILTER}\operatorname{Error}(\psi))).

These three equivalences define the following functions, called
reduction rules
:

\displaystyle f_{\land}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\psi_{1},\,\psi_{2}\}&\quad\text{if }\varphi\text{ has the form }\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},\\
\{\varphi\}&\quad\text{otherwise};\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle f_{\lor}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{aligned} \{&\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},\,\psi_{1}\land\neg\psi_{2},\,\psi_{1}\land\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}),\\
&\neg\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},\,\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})\land\psi_{2}\}\end{aligned}&\quad\text{if }\varphi\text{ has the form }\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2},\\
\{\varphi\}&\quad\text{otherwise};\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle f_{\neg}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\psi,\,\operatorname{Error}(\psi)\}&\quad\text{if }\varphi\text{ has the form }\neg\psi,\\
\{\varphi\}&\quad\text{otherwise};\end{array}\right.

Note that if the filter condition
\varphi
does not have the form of filter condition on the left side of the identity, we return the set
\{\varphi\}
. This captures the fact that the equivalence cannot be applied to reduce filter condition
\varphi
.

For convenience, we also define the reduction function that eliminates atomic formulas
f_{\circ}
and a reduction that composes
f_{\lor}
with
f_{\land}
, called
f_{\lor\land}
.

\displaystyle f_{\circ}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\{\varphi\}&\quad\text{if }\varphi\text{ is a complex filter condition},\\
\emptyset&\quad\text{if }\varphi\text{ is an atomic filter condition};\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle f_{\lor\land}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\,\neg\psi_{1},\,\neg\psi_{2},\,\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\,\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})\}&\quad\text{if }\varphi\text{ has the form }\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2},\\
\{\varphi\}&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

For
r\in\{\land,\lor,\neg,\circ,\lor\land\}
, let
F_{r}
be the function that receives a set of filter conditions
\Sigma
and returns the set of filter conditions
F_{r}(\Sigma)=\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Sigma}f_{r}(\varphi)
. Given two sets of filter conditions
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
we write
\Sigma_{1}\xrightarrow{r}\Sigma_{2}
if
F_{r}(\Sigma_{1})=\Sigma_{2}
. In this case, we say that
\Sigma_{1}\xrightarrow{r}\Sigma_{2}
is a
reduction
. We said that a filter condition
\varphi
is reducible if there is a finite sequence of reductions
\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{r_{1}}\Sigma_{1}\xrightarrow{r_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{r_{n}}\emptyset
. Intuitively, reductions are applied until all complex filter conditions are eliminated. It is not difficult to see that we can apply the aforementioned equivalences to transform every pattern
P_{1}
to a pattern
P_{2}
with no complex formulas if and only if every filter condition
\varphi
is reducible.

We next prove that every filter condition is reducible by induction on the structure of the filter condition. For this induction, we define the components of a filter condition
\varphi
, denoted
\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)
, to be the set of filter conditions defined as follows: If
\varphi
is atomic, then
\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)=\emptyset
; if
\varphi=\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}
or
\varphi=\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}
, then
\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}\cup\operatorname{comp}(\psi_{1})\cup\operatorname{comp}(\psi_{2})
; and if
\varphi=\neg\psi
then
\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)=\{\psi\}\cup\operatorname{comp}(\psi)
.

Claim 2.

Every filter condition
\varphi
is reducible.

Proof.

We prove this by induction using the following hypothesis: if
\varphi
is a filter condition where for each filter condition
\psi\in\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)
,
\psi
and
\operatorname{Error}(\psi)
are reducible, then the filter conditions
\varphi
and
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
are reducible.

(1)

If
\varphi
is
\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})
then

\displaystyle\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{\circ}\emptyset,

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)\}=\{\operatorname{\emph{false}}\}\xrightarrow{\circ}\emptyset.

(2)

If
\varphi
is
{{?}{X}}=a
then

\displaystyle\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{\circ}\emptyset,

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)\}=\{\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})\}\xrightarrow{\neg}\{\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\operatorname{Error}(\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}))\}=\{\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\operatorname{\emph{false}}\}\xrightarrow{\circ}\emptyset.

(3)

If
\varphi
is
{{?}{X}}={{?}{Y}}
then

\displaystyle\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{\circ}\emptyset,

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)\}\begin{aligned} &=\{\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})\lor\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}})\}\\
&\xrightarrow{\lor\land}\begin{aligned} \{&\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}),\;\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}),\\
&\operatorname{Error}(\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})),\;\operatorname{Error}(\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}))\}\\
\end{aligned}\\
&=\{\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}),\;\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}),\;false\}\\
&\xrightarrow{\circ}\{\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\neg\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}})\}\\
&\xrightarrow{\neg}\{\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\operatorname{Error}(\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}})),\;\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}),\;\operatorname{Error}(\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}))\}\\
&=\{\operatorname{bound}({{?}{X}}),\;\operatorname{\emph{false}},\;\operatorname{bound}({{?}{Y}}),\;\operatorname{\emph{false}}\}\\
&\xrightarrow{\circ}\emptyset.\end{aligned}

(4)

If
\varphi
is
\neg\psi_{1}
then

\displaystyle\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{\neg}\{\psi,\;\operatorname{Error}(\psi)\},

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)\}=\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi)\}.

Since
\psi\in\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)
and by inductive hypothesis, the filter conditions
\psi
and
\operatorname{Error}(\psi)
are reducible. Hence, the filter conditions
\varphi
and
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
are reducible.

(5)

If
\varphi
is
\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}
then

\displaystyle\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{\land}\{\psi_{1},\;\psi_{2}\},

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)\}\begin{aligned} &=\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi)\lor\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})\}\\
&\xrightarrow{\lor\land}\{\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\;\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}),\\
&\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\;\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}),\\
&\operatorname{Error}(\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})),\;\operatorname{Error}(\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}))\}\end{aligned}\\
&=\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\;\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}),\;\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\;\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}),\;\operatorname{\emph{false}}\}.\end{aligned}

Since
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\operatorname{comp}(\varphi)
and by the induction hypothesis, the filter conditions
\operatorname{Error}(psi_{1})
and
\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})
are reducible. To show that
\varphi
is reducible, we have to show that
\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})
and
\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})
are reducible.

\displaystyle\{\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})\}\xrightarrow{\neg}\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi),\;\operatorname{Error}(\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2}))\}=\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi),\;false\}.

By the induction hypothesis,
\operatorname{Error}(\psi)
is reducible. Hence,
\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})
is reducible. Similarly,
\neg\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})
is reducible. Then,
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is reducible.

(6)

Let
\varphi
be
\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}
. First, we show that
\varphi
is reducible.

\displaystyle\{\varphi\}\xrightarrow{\lor\land}\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\neg\psi_{1},\neg\psi_{2},\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})\}

By the induction hypothesis on
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
,
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
,
\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})
, and
\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})
are reducible. To prove that
\varphi
is reducible, suffices to prove that
\neg\psi_{1}
and
\neg\psi_{2}
are reducible.

\displaystyle\{\neg\psi_{1}\}\xrightarrow{\neg}\{\psi_{1},\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})\}.

By the induction hypothesis in
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{1}
and
\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})
are reducible. Hence,
\neg\psi_{1}
is reducible. Similarly,
\neg\psi_{2}
is reducible. Hence,
\varphi
is reducible.

Second, we show that
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is reducible.

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)\}=\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})\land\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})\}\xrightarrow{\land}\{\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1}),\;\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})\}.

By the induction hypothesis in
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
,
\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{1})
and
\operatorname{Error}(\psi_{2})
are reducible. Hence,
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
is reducible.

Hence, for every filter condition
\varphi
, the filter conditions
\varphi
and
\operatorname{Error}(\varphi)
are reducible.
∎

B.3.
Normalization of
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
queries

Claim 3 (Normalized
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
).

Let
(p(\bar{X}),\Pi)
be a
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query, and
R
be a rule in
\Pi
with form

p(\bar{X})\leftarrow A_{1},\dots,A_{m},\neg B_{1},\dots,\neg B_{n},

where
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}
are positive literals, and
\neg B_{1},\dots,\neg B_{n}
are negative literals. For
1\leq i\leq m
, let
\bar{Y}_{i}
be the set of variables that consists of the variables atoms
A_{1},\dots,A_{i}
. Consider the minimal set of rules
\Pi_{R}
that includes the following rules:

(1)

Rules
R_{i}^{A}
, for
2\leq i\leq m
, defined recursively as follows:

(a)

R_{2}^{A}=q_{2}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{2})\leftarrow A_{1},A_{2}
.

(b)

R_{i}^{A}=q_{i}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i})\leftarrow q_{i-1}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i-1}),A_{i}
.

(2)

Rules
R_{j}^{B}
for
1\leq j\leq n
,
defined recursively as follows:

(a)

R_{0}^{B}=r_{0}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow q_{m}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{m})
,

(b)

R_{j}^{B}=r_{j}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r_{j-1}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}),\neg B^{\prime}_{j}(\bar{Y}_{m})
,

(c)

R_{j}^{B^{\prime}}=B^{\prime}_{j}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r^{B}_{j-1}(\bar{Y}_{m}),B_{j}
.

(3)

A rule
R^{\prime}=p(\bar{X})\leftarrow r_{n}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}).

The
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query
(p(\bar{X}),\Pi^{\prime})
that results from replacing rule
R
in query
(p(\bar{X}),\Pi)
with the rules in
\Pi_{R}
is equivalent to query
(p(\bar{X}),\Pi)
.

Proof.

We next prove this claim by induction on the numbers
m
, of positive literals, and
n
, of negative literals, in a rule
R
. The hypothesis of induction states that the query
(q(\bar{X}),\{R\})
and its normalized query
(q(\bar{X}),\Pi_{R})
are equivalent. Since we assumed that every literal in the body of a rule must have at least one variable (see Section
4
), to guarantee safeness, the body of the rule cannot include a negative literal without having at least a positive literal.

(1)

If
m=1
and
n=0
, rule
R
is already normalized because it is the projection rule
p(\bar{X})\leftarrow A_{1}
.

(2)

If
m>1
and
n=0
then the normalization of rule
R
consists of a set
\Pi_{R}
of rules
R_{i}^{A}
, for
2\leq i\leq m
, defined recursively as follows:

\displaystyle R_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=q_{2}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{2})\leftarrow A_{1},A_{2},

\displaystyle R_{i}^{A}

\displaystyle=q_{i}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i})\leftarrow q_{i-1}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i-1}),A_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for }2\leq i\leq m,

\displaystyle R_{0}^{B}

\displaystyle=r_{0}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow q_{n}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{m}),

\displaystyle R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=p(\bar{X})\leftarrow r_{0}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}).

By the induction hypothesis, the query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{3}^{A},\dots,R_{m}^{A},R_{0}^{B},R^{\prime}\})
is equivalent to the query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R^{\prime\prime}\})
where
R^{\prime\prime}
is the rule
p(\bar{X})\leftarrow q_{2}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{2}),A_{3},\dots,A_{m}
. Hence, the query
(p(\bar{X}),\Pi_{R})
is equivalent to the query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{2}^{A},R^{\prime\prime}\})
.
To show that these queries are equivalent to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
, we need to show that they have the same answers, and each answer has the same cardinality.

Assume that a substitution
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
. Then, program
\{R\}
has a derivation tree whose root is labeled with the ground literal
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
, has
m
children labeled with the ground literals
\theta(A_{i})
, for
1\leq i\leq m
, and the edges from the root to the children are labeled with rule
R
, as is shown in Figure
5
(on the left). Then, for
1\leq i\leq m
, there is a derivation three whose root is labeled with the ground literal
\theta(A_{i})
. The existence of the ground literals
\theta(A_{i})
as labels of derivation tree roots proves that the ground literal
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
is inferred using the rules
R_{2}^{A}
and
R^{\prime\prime}
, as is shown in the Figure
5
(on the right). Then, if
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
then
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{2}^{A},R^{\prime\prime}\})
. The same argument can be used in the contrary direction to prove that if
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{2}^{A},R^{\prime\prime}\})
then
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
. Finally, the cardinality of
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
is, for both queries, the product of the cardinalities of
\theta(A_{i})
, for
1\leq i\leq m
. Hence, both queries are equivalent.

\theta(p(\bar{X}))
\theta(A_{1})
R
\theta(A_{2})
R
\theta(A_{3})
R
\dots
\theta(A_{m})
R
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
\theta(q_{2}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{2}))
\theta(A_{1})
R_{2}^{A}
\theta(A_{2})
R_{2}^{A}
R^{\prime\prime}
\theta(A_{3})
R^{\prime\prime}
\dots
\theta(A_{m})
R^{\prime\prime}

Figure 5.
Derivation trees for the ground literal
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
regarding query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
(on the left), and query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{2}^{A},R^{\prime\prime}\})
(on the right). The children of the nodes labeled with the positive ground literals
\theta(A_{i})
are omitted.

(3)

If
m>1
and
n>0
then the normalization of rule
R
consists of a set
\Pi_{R}
of rules
R_{i}^{A}
, for
2\leq i\leq m
, defined recursively as follows:

\displaystyle R_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=q_{2}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{2})\leftarrow A_{1},A_{2},

\displaystyle R_{i}^{A}

\displaystyle=q_{i}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i})\leftarrow q_{i-1}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i-1}),A_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for }2\leq i\leq m,

\displaystyle R_{0}^{B}

\displaystyle=r_{0}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow q_{m}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{m}),

\displaystyle R_{j}^{B}

\displaystyle=r_{j}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r_{j-1}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}),\neg{B^{\prime}}_{j}(\bar{Y}_{m})

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leq j\leq n,

\displaystyle R_{j}^{B^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=B^{\prime}_{j}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r^{B}_{j-1}(\bar{Y}_{m}),B_{j},

\displaystyle R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=p(\bar{X})\leftarrow r_{n}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}).

The rules above are equivalent to the following rules:

\displaystyle R_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=q_{2}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{2})\leftarrow A_{1},A_{2},

\displaystyle R_{i}^{A}

\displaystyle=q_{i}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i})\leftarrow q_{i-1}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{i-1}),A_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for }2\leq i\leq m,

\displaystyle R_{0}^{B}

\displaystyle=r_{0}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow q_{m}^{A}(\bar{Y}_{m}),

\displaystyle R_{j}^{B}

\displaystyle=r_{j}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r_{j-1}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}),\neg B^{\prime}_{j}(\bar{Y}_{m})

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leq j\leq n-1,

\displaystyle R_{j}^{B^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=B^{\prime}_{j}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r^{B}_{j-1}(\bar{Y}_{m}),B_{j},

\displaystyle R_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=t(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow r_{n-1}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}),

\displaystyle R_{\beta}

\displaystyle=r_{n}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow t(\bar{Y}_{m}),\neg B_{n},

\displaystyle R^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=p(\bar{X})\leftarrow r_{n}^{B}(\bar{Y}_{m}).

By the induction hypothesis, the query
(t(\bar{Y}_{m}),(\Pi_{R}\cup\{R_{\alpha}\})\setminus\{R_{n}^{B},R^{\prime}\})
is equivalent to the query
(t(\bar{Y}_{m}),\{R_{\gamma}\})
where
R_{\gamma}
is the rule
t(\bar{Y}_{m})\leftarrow A_{1},\dots,A_{m},B_{1},\dots,B_{n-1}
. Hence, the query
(p(\bar{X}),\Pi_{R})
is equivalent to the query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{\gamma},R_{\beta},R^{\prime\prime}\})
To show that these queries are equivalent to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
, we need to show that they have the same answers, and each answer has the same cardinalities.

Assume that a substitution
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
. Then, program
\{R\}
has a derivation tree whose root is labeled with the ground literal
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
, has
m
children labeled with the ground literals
\theta(A_{i})
, and
n
children labeled with literals
\neg\theta(B_{j})
, for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, and the edges from the root to the children are labeled with rule
R
, as is shown in Figure
6
(on the left). Then, for
1\leq i\leq m
, there is a derivation three whose root is labeled with the ground literal
\theta(A_{i})
, and for
1\leq j\leq n
, there is no derivation three whose root is labeled with the ground literal
\theta(B_{j})
. The existence of the ground literals
\theta(A_{i})
and the non-existence of the ground literals
\theta(B_{j})
as labels of derivation tree roots prove that the ground literal
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
is inferred using the rules
R_{\gamma}
,
R_{\beta}
, and
R^{\prime\prime}
, as is shown in the Figure
6
(on the right). Then, if
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
then
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{\gamma},R_{\beta},R^{\prime\prime}\})
. The same argument can be used in the contrary direction to prove that if
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R_{\gamma},R_{\beta},R^{\prime\prime}\})
then
\theta
is an answer to query
(p(\bar{X}),\{R\})
. Finally, the cardinality of
\theta(p(\bar{X}))
is, for both queries, the product of the cardinalities of
\theta(A_{i})
, for
1\leq i\leq m
. Hence, both queries are equivalent.

Hence, we have proved that the normalization method produces an equivalent
\text{NRMD}^{\neg}
query.
∎
